\label{rem:balls-vs-cylinders}
The Vitali lemma with the $5r$ enlargement is a statement about balls of a
metric space, and the parabolic cylinders $\Cyl{r}{z}$ are not balls of
$\dpar$. Two intersecting backward cylinders need not be contained in
one another's enlargements about the original top centres: a point later
than one top time remains outside every cylinder with that top time.
We therefore run the covering with the genuine $\dpar$-balls
$\mathfrak{B}_{r}(z)$, and recover the statement about cylinders through
Lemma~\ref{lem:P1-cylinders}. The defect inequality \eqref{eq:defect-r} is still
stated on cylinders, which is where the local energy inequality lives, and the
inclusion $\Cyl{r}{z}\subset\mathfrak{B}_{r}(z)$ of
Lemma~\ref{lem:parabolic-metric} is what links the two. An alternative used in
\cite[\S6.2, pp.~96--97]{Tsai2018} uses the shifted cylinders
$Q^*(z,r)=B_r(x)\times(t-7r^2/8,t+r^2/8)$, which contain both $z$ and
$\Cyl{r/2}{z}$.
\end{remark}

%======================================================================
\part*{Global existence of suitable Leray--Hopf solutions}
%======================================================================

The remaining sections construct, for every divergence-free $L^2$ datum, a
global Leray--Hopf solution that is a suitable weak solution in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:sws}, together with its associated pressure, and their
versions with a force. The Caffarelli--Kohn--Nirenberg theorem,
Theorem~\ref{thm:C}, then applies to these solutions. The results are stated
in Section~\ref{sec:leray-hopf}, the pressure is constructed in
Section~\ref{sec:riesz-pressure}, and the solutions in
Sections~\ref{sec:regularised}--\ref{sec:forced-leray}.

\section{Solution classes and main results}\label{sec:leray-hopf}

This section fixes the solution notions and states the results of this part.
The suitable weak solutions are those of Definition~\ref{def:sws}; the
Leray--Hopf solutions transcribe (1.3)--(1.7) of~\cite{ESS2003}.

\paragraph{Velocity and gradient as data.}
In this part a velocity is a map $u\colon\R^3\times\R\to\R^3$, and its
weak spatial gradient is carried as separate data
$Du\colon\R^3\times\R\to\R^{3\times3}$ with $Du_{ij}=\partial_j u_i$, as in
Remark~\ref{rem:gradient-datum}; for open $\Omega\subset\R^3$ and an open
interval $I\subset\R$, $\Cc(\Omega\times I)$ denotes the smooth functions with
compact support in $\Omega\times I$. Let $q>\nfs52$ and
$f\in\Lloc{q}(\Omega\times I;\R^3)$. The quadruple $(u,Du,p,f)$ is a
\emph{suitable weak solution on $\Omega\times I$} if $(u,p)$ is a suitable
weak solution with force $f$ on $\Omega\times I$ in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:sws}, with $Du$ in the role of $\nabla u$. Explicitly,
conditions (S1)--(S4) of Definition~\ref{def:sws} then read as follows.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(S\arabic*)]
\item For every open $\Omega'$ with compact closure
  $\overline{\Omega'}\subset\Omega$ and every interval $J$ with compact closure
  $\overline J\subset I$: $u$, $Du$, $p$, $f$ are measurable on
  $\Omega'\times J$;
  $\esssup_{t\in J}\int_{\Omega'}\abs{u(x,t)}^2\,dx<\infty$;
  $\iint_{\Omega'\times J}\abs{u}^2+\abs{Du}^2<\infty$;
  $p\in L^{3/2}(\Omega'\times J)$; $f\in L^q(\Omega'\times J)$; and for
  a.e.\ $t\in J$, $Du(\cdot,t)$ is the weak gradient of $u(\cdot,t)$ on
  $\Omega'$.
\item $\iint u\cdot\nabla\psi=0$ for every $\psi\in\Cc(\Omega\times I)$.
\item For every $\varphi\in\Cc(\Omega\times I;\R^3)$,
  \[
  \iint\Bigl[-u\cdot\dt\varphi-u_iu_j\,\partial_j\varphi_i+Du_{ij}\,\partial_j\varphi_i
    -p\,\dv\varphi-f\cdot\varphi\Bigr]=0 .
  \]
\item For every $\psi\in\Cc(\Omega\times I)$ with $\psi\ge0$,
  \[
  2\iint\abs{Du}^2\psi\le\iint\Bigl[\abs{u}^2(\dt\psi+\Delta\psi)
    +(\abs{u}^2+2p)\,u\cdot\nabla\psi+2(f\cdot u)\,\psi\Bigr].
  \]
\end{enumerate}
Regular points, the singular set and the parabolic Hausdorff measure are those
of Definitions~\ref{def:regular} and~\ref{def:parabolic-hausdorff}.

\paragraph{Divergence-free data.}
Following~\cite{ESS2003}, $\J$ is the closure in $L^2(\R^3;\R^3)$ of the
smooth compactly supported divergence-free fields. We use the equivalent
description by the weak divergence.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:J-weak-div}
A field $a\in L^2(\R^3;\R^3)$ belongs to $\J$ if and only if
$\int_{\R^3}a\cdot\nabla\psi=0$ for every $\psi\in\Cc(\R^3)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:J-weak-div}]
If $a\in\J$, choose $a_k\in C_c^\infty(\R^3;\R^3)$ with
$\dv a_k=0$ and $a_k\to a$ in $L^2$. For $\psi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$,
integration by parts gives $\int a_k\cdot\nabla\psi=0$, and
$\abs{\int(a_k-a)\cdot\nabla\psi}\le
\norm{a_k-a}_2
\norm{\nabla\psi}_2\to0$. This proves the weak-divergence
identity.

Conversely, suppose that $a\in L^2$ satisfies that identity. With the
Fourier convention $\widehat f(\xi)=\int e^{-2\pi i x\cdot\xi}f(x)\,dx$,
for $\delta>0$ define a vector potential $A_\delta$ and its curl $b_\delta$
by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-vector-potential}
 \widehat{A_\delta}(\xi)=
   \frac{i\,\xi\times\widehat a(\xi)}{2\pi(|\xi|^2+\delta^2)},
 \qquad
 \widehat{b_\delta}(\xi)=
   \frac{|\xi|^2}{|\xi|^2+\delta^2}\widehat a(\xi).
\end{equation}
The weak divergence identity gives $\xi\cdot\widehat a(\xi)=0$ almost
everywhere, so $b_\delta=\curl A_\delta$. The multipliers show that
$A_\delta$ and its first weak derivatives belong to $L^2$. Spatially
mollifying this $H^1$ potential gives smooth $L^2$ potentials whose curls
converge to $b_\delta$ in $L^2$; cutting off each smooth potential on
expanding balls shows that its curl belongs to $\J$. Thus $b_\delta\in\J$.

For $\delta_n=1/(n+1)$ the curl multiplier is bounded by one and converges
to one almost everywhere. Plancherel and dominated convergence therefore give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-cutoff-convergence}
 \|b_{\delta_n}-a\|_2\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
The closedness of $\J$ in $L^2$ now gives $a\in\J$. This route regularizes
the Fourier potential and passes to its curl in $L^2$; it does not require an
$L^6$ potential estimate or a prescribed cutoff-curl sequence for $a$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Leray--Hopf solutions.}
The velocity of a Leray--Hopf solution is a function defined at every time,
and it is this function, not an equivalence class, that the definition
constrains at every time. The same function is used as the velocity of a
suitable weak solution.

\begin{definition}[Leray--Hopf solution]\label{def:leray-hopf}
Let $0<T<\infty$ and $a\in\J$. The pair $(u,Du)$ is a \emph{Leray--Hopf
solution on $\R^3\times[0,T]$ with initial datum $a$} if the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(LH\arabic*)]
\item\label{LH:class} \emph{Energy class, \cite[(1.3)]{ESS2003}.} $u$ and $Du$
  are measurable on $\R^3\times(0,T)$;
  $\esssup_{t\in(0,T)}\int\abs{u(x,t)}^2dx<\infty$;
  $\iint_{\R^3\times(0,T)}\abs{u}^2+\abs{Du}^2<\infty$; and for a.e.\
  $t\in(0,T)$, $Du(\cdot,t)$ is the weak gradient of $u(\cdot,t)$ on $\R^3$
  and $\int u(x,t)\cdot\nabla\psi(x)\,dx=0$ for every $\psi\in\Cc(\R^3)$.
\item\label{LH:weakcont} \emph{Weak continuity, \cite[(1.4)]{ESS2003}.} For
  every $w\in L^2(\R^3;\R^3)$ the map $t\mapsto\int u(x,t)\cdot w(x)\,dx$ is
  continuous on $[0,T]$.
\item\label{LH:eq} \emph{Weak equation, \cite[(1.5)]{ESS2003}.} For every
  $\varphi\in\Cc(\R^3\times(0,T);\R^3)$ with $\dv\varphi=0$,
  \[
  \iint\Bigl[-u\cdot\dt\varphi-u_iu_j\,\partial_j\varphi_i+Du_{ij}\,\partial_j\varphi_i\Bigr]=0 .
  \]
\item\label{LH:energy} \emph{Energy inequality, \cite[(1.6)]{ESS2003}.} For
  every $t_0\in[0,T]$,
  \[
  \frac12\int\abs{u(x,t_0)}^2dx+\int_0^{t_0}\!\!\int\abs{Du}^2\,dx\,dt\le\frac12\int\abs{a}^2dx .
  \]
\item\label{LH:initial} \emph{Initial datum, \cite[(1.7)]{ESS2003}.}
  $\int\abs{u(x,t)-a(x)}^2dx\to0$ as $t\downarrow0$.
\end{enumerate}
A \emph{global Leray--Hopf solution} with datum $a$ is a pair $(u,Du)$ that is
a Leray--Hopf solution on $\R^3\times[0,T]$ for every $T>0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[The global notion]\label{rem:global-LH}
\cite{ESS2003} define the global notion by replacing $[0,T]$ with $[0,\infty)$
in (1.3)--(1.7). Read literally, (1.3) would then require
$u\in L^2(0,\infty;W^{1,2})$, that is, $\int_0^\infty\!\int\abs{u}^2<\infty$,
which Leray's solutions need not satisfy; the energy inequality controls
only $\sup_t\int\abs{u}^2$ and $\int_0^\infty\!\int\abs{\nabla u}^2$. We therefore
define global solutions through every finite interval, which is the reading
under which~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{ESS2003} holds.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Main results.}
The first theorem is Leray's existence theorem, with the constructed
solution suitable in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:sws}.

\begin{theorem}[Existence]\label{thm:leray}
For every $a\in\J$ there are $u$, $Du$ and $p$ such that $(u,Du)$ is a global
Leray--Hopf solution with datum $a$, and $(u,Du,p,0)$ is a suitable weak
solution on $\R^3\times(0,\infty)$ for every $q>\nf52$.
\end{theorem}

Combined with the Caffarelli--Kohn--Nirenberg theorem, Theorem~\ref{thm:C},
this gives the headline of~\cite{CKN1982}.

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:ckn-headline}
For every $a\in\J$ there is a global Leray--Hopf solution $(u,Du)$ with datum
$a$ whose singular set in $\R^3\times(0,\infty)$ has zero one-dimensional
parabolic Hausdorff measure.
\end{corollary}

Every Leray--Hopf solution has a pressure.

\begin{theorem}[Associated pressure]\label{thm:assoc-pressure}
Let $(u,Du)$ be a Leray--Hopf solution on $\R^3\times[0,T]$ with datum
$a\in\J$. There is $p\in L^{5/3}(\R^3\times(0,T))$ such that (S3) of
Definition~\ref{def:sws} holds on $\R^3\times(0,T)$ with $f=0$. If in
addition $\esssup_{t\in(0,T)}\norm{u(\cdot,t)}_{L^3(\R^3)}<\infty$, then
this $p$ also belongs to $L^\infty((0,T);L^{3/2}(\R^3))$.
\end{theorem}

\paragraph{Forced Leray--Hopf solutions.}
The same all-time velocity representative is used in the forced equation,
the work term, and the initial trace.

\begin{definition}[Forced Leray--Hopf solution]\label{def:forced-leray-hopf}
Let $0<T<\infty$, $a\in\J$, and
$f\in L^2(\R^3\times(0,T);\R^3)$. The pair $(u,Du)$ is a
\emph{forced Leray--Hopf solution on $\R^3\times[0,T]$ with datum $a$ and
force $f$} if it satisfies (LH1), (LH2), and (LH5) of
Definition~\ref{def:leray-hopf}, and also the following two clauses:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(FLH\arabic*)]
\item For every $\varphi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3\times(0,T);\R^3)$ with
  $\dv\varphi=0$,
  \[
  \iint\Bigl[-u\cdot\partial_t\varphi-u_i u_j\,\partial_j\varphi_i
       +Du_{ij}\,\partial_j\varphi_i-f\cdot\varphi\Bigr]\,dx\,dt=0\,.
  \]
\item For every $t\in[0,T]$,
  \[
  \frac12\int_{\R^3}|u(x,t)|^2\,dx
  +\int_0^t\!\int_{\R^3}|Du|^2\,dx\,ds
  \le\frac12\int_{\R^3}|a|^2\,dx
  +\int_0^t\!\int_{\R^3}f\cdot u\,dx\,ds\,.
  \]
\end{enumerate}
A \emph{global forced Leray--Hopf solution} is one pair $(u,Du)$ that is
a forced Leray--Hopf solution on every finite interval, with the same
representative and force.
\end{definition}

The force work is an ordinary Lebesgue integral: on every finite slab,
$u\in L^\infty_tL^2_x$ and $f\in L^2_{t,x}$, so $f\cdot u\in L^1_{t,x}$.

\begin{theorem}[Forced suitable existence]\label{thm:leray-forced}
Let $a\in\J$ and let $f\colon\R^3\times(0,\infty)\to\R^3$ satisfy
$f\in L^2(\R^3\times(0,T))$ for every finite $T$ and
$f\in L^{q_0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\R^3\times(0,\infty))$ for some
$q_0>5/2$. There are one velocity $u$, weak gradient $Du$, and pressure
$p$ such that $(u,Du)$ is a global forced Leray--Hopf solution with datum
$a$. For every $q>5/2$ for which
$f\in L^q_{\mathrm{loc}}(\R^3\times(0,\infty))$, the same quadruple
$(u,Du,p,f)$ is a suitable weak solution on
$\R^3\times(0,\infty)$ in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:sws}. In particular, its local energy inequality
contains the term $2(f\cdot u)\psi$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Forced singular-set conclusion]
\label{thm:leray-forced-singularSet}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:leray-forced}, for every
$q>5/2$ for which $f\in L^q_{\mathrm{loc}}$, the witness there satisfies
\[
 \Phaus^{1}\bigl(S(u)\bigr)=0\,.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Forced associated pressure]\label{thm:assoc-pressure-forced}
Let $(u,Du)$ be a forced Leray--Hopf solution on
$\R^3\times[0,T]$ with datum $a\in\J$ and
$f\in L^2(\R^3\times(0,T);\R^3)$. Let
$p_N=P[u\otimes u]$ and let $p_f$ be the force pressure of
Lemma~\ref{lem:force-pressure}. Then
\[
 p_N\in L^{5/3}(\R^3\times(0,T)),\qquad
 p_f\in L^2((0,T);L^6(\R^3)),\qquad
 p=p_N+p_f\in L^{3/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\R^3\times(0,T)).
\]
For every $\varphi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3\times(0,T);\R^3)$,
\[
 \iint\Bigl[-u\cdot\partial_t\varphi-u_i u_j\,\partial_j\varphi_i
       +Du_{ij}\,\partial_j\varphi_i-p\,\dv\varphi
       -f\cdot\varphi\Bigr]\,dx\,dt=0\,.
\]
When $f=0$, this pressure is the pressure of Theorem~\ref{thm:assoc-pressure}
and has all of its stated global and conditional integrability properties.
\end{theorem}

\section{Riesz transforms and the associated pressure}\label{sec:riesz-pressure}

The weak equation in the Leray--Hopf definition is tested only against
solenoidal fields. We first construct the whole-space pressure that extends
that equation to arbitrary compactly supported vector tests. Its sign is fixed
by the convention that the pressure term in (S3) is $-p\,\dv\varphi$.

\begin{definition}[Whole-space pressure]\label{def:riesz-pressure}
For $1<r<\infty$, on Schwartz functions let $\Riesz{j}$ denote the Fourier
multiplier $i\xi_j/|\xi|$. The product $\Riesz{i}\Riesz{j}$ has a bounded
extension to $L^r(\R^3)$. For a jointly measurable tensor field $F=(F_{ij})$
whose slices $F_{ij}(\cdot,t)$ belong to $L^r(\R^3)$ for almost every $t$,
define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:riesz-pressure}
P[F](\cdot,t)=\sum_{i,j=1}^3 \Riesz{i}\Riesz{j}F_{ij}(\cdot,t).
\end{equation}
The extensions for different exponents agree on their common domains, so this
defines one pressure operator. Indeed, bounded compactly supported inputs
belong to $L^r\cap L^s\cap L^2$ for any two finite exponents $r,s$; the
extensions of External Input~\ref{ext:CZ} agree there. Truncating an
input in $L^r\cap L^s$ by balls and bounded values gives convergence in both
norms, so the two outputs agree as distributions. With this convention
\begin{equation}\label{eq:riesz-pressure-sign}
-\Delta P[F]=\sum_{i,j=1}^3\partial_i\partial_jF_{ij}
\end{equation}
in the spatial distribution sense for almost every $t$. Here $\Riesz{i}\Riesz{j}$ is the operator $R_iR_j$ of
External Input~\ref{ext:CZ}, the bounded extension of
$\partial_i\partial_j(-\Delta)^{-1}$.

There is a jointly measurable representative of $P[F]$, and for almost every
$t$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:riesz-slice-bound}
\norm{P[F](\cdot,t)}_{L^r(\R^3)}
\le C_r\sum_{i,j=1}^3\norm{F_{ij}(\cdot,t)}_{L^r(\R^3)}.
\end{equation}
Here and below $C_r$ depends only on $r$ in dimension three. If
$F\in L^r(\R^3\times(0,T))$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:riesz-spacetime-bound}
\norm{P[F]}_{L^r(\R^3\times(0,T))}
\le C_r\sum_{i,j=1}^3\norm{F_{ij}}_{L^r(\R^3\times(0,T))}.
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

The bounded extensions are supplied by External Input~\ref{ext:CZ}. For joint measurability, set
$E_m=\{t:\sum_{i,j}\norm{F_{ij}(\cdot,t)}_r\le m\}$.
Fubini's theorem gives a strongly measurable $L^r(\R^3)$-valued slice map on
each $E_m$, since $F$ restricted to $\R^3\times E_m$ belongs to the product
$L^r$ space. Applying the bounded operator to simple-function
approximations gives a strongly measurable pressure map there, and hence a
jointly measurable representative. The sets $E_m$ cover almost every time.
The slice estimate follows from the nine operator bounds; integrating its
$r$th power gives the space-time estimate. The sign in
\eqref{eq:riesz-pressure-sign} follows from the multiplier
$-\xi_i\xi_j/|\xi|^2$ of $\Riesz{i}\Riesz{j}$.

\begin{lemma}[Riesz duality]\label{lem:riesz-duality}
Let $1<r<\infty$, $r'=r/(r-1)$, $F\in L^r(\R^3\times(0,T))$, and
$g\in L^{r'}(\R^3\times(0,T))$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:riesz-duality}
\iint P[F]g=\sum_{i,j=1}^3\iint F_{ij}\,\Riesz{j}\Riesz{i}g.
\end{equation}
In particular, suppose that $F\in L^{3/2}(\R^3\times(0,T))$ and that
$\psi\in C^\infty(\R^3\times(0,T))$ is supported in a compact time interval
and satisfies, for every spatial multi-index $\alpha$ with $|\alpha|\le2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:riesz-potential-decay}
|\partial_x^\alpha\psi(x,t)|\le C_\alpha(1+|x|)^{-2-|\alpha|}
\quad\text{for all }(x,t),
\end{equation}
with constants independent of $t$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:riesz-noncompact-duality}
\iint P[F]\,\Delta\psi
=-\sum_{i,j=1}^3\iint F_{ij}\,\partial_i\partial_j\psi.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
On Schwartz functions, the multiplier $-\xi_i\xi_j/|\xi|^2$ is real and
even, so $\Riesz{i}\Riesz{j}$ is self-adjoint and commutes with
$\Riesz{j}\Riesz{i}$. This proves the scalar duality on Schwartz pairs.
Density in $L^r$ and $L^{r'}$, together with the operator bounds and
Hölder's inequality, extends it to each pair of $L^r$ and $L^{r'}$
functions. Apply this at almost every time and integrate; Hölder's
inequality on space-time makes both sides integrable and proves
\eqref{eq:riesz-duality}.

For the particular identity, first take $\psi$ smooth and compactly
supported in space. The multiplier identity
$\Riesz{j}\Riesz{i}\Delta\psi=-\partial_i\partial_j\psi$ gives
\eqref{eq:riesz-noncompact-duality}. For a $\psi$ satisfying
\eqref{eq:riesz-potential-decay}, choose $\chi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$ equal to
one on $B_1$, supported in $B_2$, and put $\chi_R(x)=\chi(x/R)$. The decay
in \eqref{eq:riesz-potential-decay} implies
$\chi_R\psi\to\psi$ in $L^3$ together with all spatial derivatives of order
at most two: the cubed tail bounds decay respectively as
$(1+|x|)^{-6}$, $(1+|x|)^{-9}$, and $(1+|x|)^{-12}$, each integrable on
$\R^3$. Terms where a derivative falls on $\chi_R$ are supported in
$R\le|x|\le2R$; a term of total derivative order $k\le2$ is bounded there
by $C R^{-2-k}$, so its cubed integral is $O(R^{-3-3k})$. Thus
$\Delta(\chi_R\psi)\to\Delta\psi$ and
$\partial_i\partial_j(\chi_R\psi)\to\partial_i\partial_j\psi$ in
$L^3(\R^3\times(0,T))$. Apply the compactly supported identity to
$\chi_R\psi$ and pass to the limit by Hölder's inequality with
$P[F],F\in L^{3/2}$. This proves
\eqref{eq:riesz-noncompact-duality}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Helmholtz decomposition of a test field]
\label{lem:helmholtz-test}
Let $\varphi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3\times(0,T);\R^3)$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:helmholtz-potential}
\psi(x,t)=-\frac1{4\pi}\int_{\R^3}
\frac{\dv\varphi(y,t)}{|x-y|}\,dy,
\qquad \varphi^\sigma=\varphi-\nabla\psi.
\end{equation}
Then $\Delta\psi=\dv\varphi$, $\dv\varphi^\sigma=0$, and
$\varphi^\sigma=\curl A$, where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:helmholtz-vector-potential}
A(x,t)=\frac1{4\pi}\int_{\R^3}
\frac{\curl\varphi(y,t)}{|x-y|}\,dy.
\end{equation}
For every integer $k\ge0$, there are constants depending only on
$\varphi$ and $k$ such that
$|D_x^k\psi(x,t)|+|D_x^k\partial_t\psi(x,t)|\le
C_k(1+|x|)^{-2-k}$ and
$|D_x^k A(x,t)|+|D_x^k\partial_tA(x,t)|\le
C_k(1+|x|)^{-2-k}$. Let $\chi$ be as in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:riesz-duality}, set $\chi_R(x)=\chi(x/R)$, and define
$w_R=\curl(\chi_RA)$. Then $w_R$ is smooth, compactly supported in space,
divergence-free, and has the same compact time support as $\varphi$. As
$R\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:helmholtz-cutoff-norms}
\begin{aligned}
&\norm{\partial_t(w_R-\varphi^\sigma)}_{L^1_tL^2_x}
+\norm{\nabla(w_R-\varphi^\sigma)}_{L^2_{t,x}}
+\norm{\nabla(w_R-\varphi^\sigma)}_{L^1_tL^\infty_x}
\longrightarrow 0.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These are the norms needed to pass all three terms of (LH3) under (LH1).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The spatial supports of $\varphi$ and all its time derivatives lie in one
fixed ball. Each component of $\dv\varphi$ and $\curl\varphi$ has integral
zero, by integration of a compactly supported derivative. For a compactly
supported smooth function $h$ with integral zero, differentiating its
Newtonian potential and subtracting $D^k(|x|^{-1})\int h=0$ gives
$|D^k(|\cdot|^{-1}*h)(x)|\le C_k(1+|x|)^{-2-k}$ outside twice the support;
inside that ball smoothness gives the same bound after increasing $C_k$.
Apply this argument to $\dv\varphi$, $\curl\varphi$, and their time
derivatives to obtain the stated estimates. The fundamental solution
identities give $\Delta\psi=\dv\varphi$ and
$-\Delta A=\curl\varphi$. Since
$\curl\curl\varphi=\nabla\dv\varphi-\Delta\varphi$,
\begin{equation*}
\curl A=(-\Delta)^{-1}\curl\curl\varphi
=(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\dv\varphi+(-\Delta)^{-1}(-\Delta\varphi)
=-\nabla\psi+\varphi.
\end{equation*}
Here $(-\Delta)^{-1}\dv\varphi=-\psi$ by
$\Delta\psi=\dv\varphi$; the decay at infinity fixes the Newtonian
representatives in these identities. This proves
$\curl A=\varphi^\sigma$, and $\dv\varphi^\sigma=0$ follows from
$\Delta\psi=\dv\varphi$.

The cutoff field $w_R$ is compactly supported and smooth, and its divergence
vanishes because it is a curl. The identity
$w_R-\varphi^\sigma=\curl((\chi_R-1)A)$ and the derivative bounds on $A$
show, for $R\ge1$, that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:helmholtz-cutoff-estimates}
\norm{\partial_t(w_R-\varphi^\sigma)}_{L^1_tL^2_x}
 +\norm{\nabla(w_R-\varphi^\sigma)}_{L^2_{t,x}}
 \le C_\varphi R^{-3/2},\qquad
\norm{\nabla(w_R-\varphi^\sigma)}_{L^1_tL^\infty_x}
 \le C_\varphi R^{-3}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, outside $B_R$ the time derivative error is bounded by a constant
times $(1+|x|)^{-3}$; the gradient error is bounded by a constant times
$(1+|x|)^{-4}$, including the annular terms where a derivative lands on
$\chi_R$. Their $L^2$ tails and spatial suprema give
\eqref{eq:helmholtz-cutoff-estimates}. The time support has finite measure.
This proves \eqref{eq:helmholtz-cutoff-norms}.

For the passage in (LH3), (LH1) gives $u\in L^\infty_tL^2_x$,
$Du\in L^2_{t,x}$, and
$u\otimes u\in L^\infty_tL^1_x$. Thus the errors in the time, gradient,
and quadratic terms are bounded respectively by
$\norm{u}_{L^\infty_tL^2_x}\norm{\partial_t(w_R-\varphi^\sigma)}_{L^1_tL^2_x}$,
$\norm{Du}_{L^2_{t,x}}\norm{\nabla(w_R-\varphi^\sigma)}_{L^2_{t,x}}$, and
$\norm{u\otimes u}_{L^\infty_tL^1_x}
\norm{\nabla(w_R-\varphi^\sigma)}_{L^1_tL^\infty_x}$.
Each tends to zero by \eqref{eq:helmholtz-cutoff-estimates}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Energy interpolation]\label{lem:u-ten-thirds}
If $(u,Du)$ satisfies (LH1) and (LH4) on $\R^3\times[0,T]$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:u-ten-thirds}
\iint_{\R^3\times(0,T)}|u|^{10/3}
\le C\left(\esssup_{t\in(0,T)}\norm{u(\cdot,t)}_2\right)^{4/3}
\iint_{\R^3\times(0,T)}|Du|^2
\le C\norm{a}_2^{10/3}.
\end{equation}
Consequently $u\otimes u\in L^{5/3}(\R^3\times(0,T))$. Also
$u\in L^3(\R^3\times(0,T))$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:u-three}
\iint_{\R^3\times(0,T)}|u|^3
\le C T^{1/4}\norm{a}_2^{3}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For almost every $t$, (LH1) gives $u(\cdot,t)\in H^1(\R^3)$ with weak
gradient $Du(\cdot,t)$. Interpolation between $L^2$ and $L^6$ in space,
with interpolation weight $3/5$ on $L^6$, and the Sobolev inequality give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gn-ten-thirds}
\norm{u(\cdot,t)}_{10/3}
\le \norm{u(\cdot,t)}_2^{2/5}\norm{u(\cdot,t)}_6^{3/5}
\le C\norm{u(\cdot,t)}_2^{2/5}\norm{Du(\cdot,t)}_2^{3/5}.
\end{equation}
Raising \eqref{eq:gn-ten-thirds} to the power $10/3$ gives the integrand
bound with exponents $4/3$ and $2$.
Integrating and using (LH4) at $t_0=T$ proves the first inequality in
\eqref{eq:u-ten-thirds}. The same energy inequality gives
$\esssup_t\norm{u(\cdot,t)}_2\le\norm{a}_2$ and
$\iint|Du|^2\le\frac12\norm{a}_2^2$, proving the second. Since the
Frobenius norm satisfies $|u\otimes u|=|u|^2$,
$\norm{u\otimes u}_{5/3}=\norm{u}_{10/3}^2$.

Interpolation between $L^2$ and $L^6$ with equal weights, followed by the
Sobolev inequality on $\R^3$, gives
$\norm{u(\cdot,t)}_3\le \norm{u(\cdot,t)}_2^{1/2}
\norm{u(\cdot,t)}_6^{1/2}\le C\norm{u(\cdot,t)}_2^{1/2}
\norm{Du(\cdot,t)}_2^{1/2}$. Cubing this estimate and applying Hölder's
inequality in time yields
\begin{equation*}
\int_0^T\norm{Du(\cdot,t)}_2^{3/2}\,dt
\le T^{1/4}\left(\int_0^T\norm{Du(\cdot,t)}_2^2\,dt\right)^{3/4}.
\end{equation*}
Using (LH4) once more proves \eqref{eq:u-three}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:assoc-pressure}]
Set $F_{ij}=u_i u_j$ and $p=P[F]$. Lemma~\ref{lem:u-ten-thirds} and
\eqref{eq:riesz-spacetime-bound} at $r=5/3$ give
$p\in L^{5/3}(\R^3\times(0,T))$. For almost every $t$, the Sobolev
inequality and (LH1) give $u(\cdot,t)\in L^6\cap L^2$; interpolation gives
$u(\cdot,t)\in L^4$, so $F(\cdot,t)\in L^2$. Thus the $r=3/2$ pressure
used in Lemma~\ref{lem:riesz-duality} and the $r=5/3$ pressure just defined
agree almost everywhere by their common $L^2$ realization.

Fix $\varphi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^3\times(0,T);\mathbb R^3)$ and take its Helmholtz potentials $A,\psi$ from Lemma~\ref{lem:helmholtz-test}, so $\varphi=\curl A+\nabla\psi$. Let $\chi_n$ be the expanding spatial cutoffs used there, and set
\[
 V_n=\curl(\chi_n A),\qquad g_n=\chi_n\psi,\qquad
 \Phi_n=V_n+\nabla g_n.
\]
These are compactly supported smooth tests, $\dv V_n=0$, and $\Phi_n\to\varphi$ pointwise with their first derivatives. Applying (LH3) to $V_n$ removes its pressure term. For the compact gradient test $\nabla g_n$, its contribution to (S3) is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pressure-gradient-test}
\iint\left[-u_i\partial_t\partial_i g_n
-F_{ij}\partial_j\partial_i g_n
+Du_{ij}\partial_j\partial_i g_n
-p\Delta g_n\right].
\end{equation}
The time term is zero by weak divergence-freeness in (LH1), applied to $\partial_tg_n$. The weak-gradient identity and then (LH1), both with compactly supported scalar tests, give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:viscous-gradient-test}
\iint Du_{ij}\partial_j\partial_i g_n
=-\iint u_i\partial_i\Delta g_n=0.
\end{equation}
Compact-test Riesz duality gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pressure-gradient-cancellation}
\iint p\Delta g_n=-\iint F_{ij}\partial_i\partial_j g_n,
\end{equation}
so the nonlinear and pressure terms in~\eqref{eq:pressure-gradient-test} cancel. Thus the momentum functional vanishes on each $\Phi_n$.

It remains to pass to the given test in one limit. If $J\Subset(0,T)$ contains the time support of $\varphi$, put $S(x,t)=\mathbf1_J(t)(1+|x|)^{-2}$. The decay estimates for the Newtonian potentials and the cutoff derivative bounds give a common bound $C_\varphi S$ for every first derivative of $\Phi_n$; these derivatives converge pointwise to those of $\varphi$. Therefore the absolute value of the entire momentum integrand for $\Phi_n$ is bounded by the single integrable function
\[
 C_\varphi S(x,t)\bigl(|u|+|F|+|Du|+|p|\bigr).
\]
Indeed, $S\in L^2\cap L^{5/2}$, while $u,Du\in L^2$ and $F,p\in L^{5/3}$. Dominated convergence applied once to the full sum of terms proves (S3) for $\varphi$, establishing the first assertion.

The second assertion, for the same
$p=P[u\otimes u]$, is proved in Lemma~\ref{lem:assoc-pressure-L3}; that
lemma uses only the first assertion and the bounds of Definition~\ref{def:riesz-pressure}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Departure from \cite{ESS2003}]
The Leray--Hopf weak equation (1.5) of~\cite[p.~212]{ESS2003} is stated only for
solenoidal tests. Here we identify the canonical whole-space pressure by a
double Riesz transform and prove the corresponding identity for arbitrary
compactly supported vector tests. The Helmholtz decomposition and the
noncompact-potential cutoff argument supply this pressure extension and fix
its sign relative to (S3).
\end{remark}


%======================================================================

\begin{lemma}[Pressure bound under the critical velocity hypothesis]
\label{lem:assoc-pressure-L3}
Let $(u,Du)$ be a Leray--Hopf solution on $\R^3\times[0,T]$ with datum
$a\in\J$, and suppose that
$\esssup_{t\in(0,T)}\int_{\R^3}\abs{u(x,t)}^3dx<\infty$. Let
$p_*=P[u\otimes u]$ be the pressure witness
selected in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:assoc-pressure}. Then
$p_*\in L^{5/3}(\R^3\times(0,T))$, the identity (S3) of
Definition~\ref{def:sws} holds with $p=p_*$ and $f=0$, and
$p_*\in L^\infty((0,T);L^{3/2}(\R^3))$. There is a constant $C$ depending
only on the dimension and the $L^{3/2}$ double-Riesz-transform bound such that
\[
 \esssup_{t\in(0,T)}\norm{p_*(\cdot,t)}_{L^{3/2}(\R^3)}
 \le C\left(\esssup_{t\in(0,T)}
                 \norm{u(\cdot,t)}_{L^3(\R^3)}\right)^2.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:assoc-pressure} takes
$F_{ij}=u_i u_j$ and sets $p_*=P[F]$. Lemma~\ref{lem:u-ten-thirds}
gives $F\in L^{5/3}(\R^3\times(0,T))$; the proof there uses
Lemma~\ref{lem:riesz-duality} to verify the gradient part of (S3). Thus the
existential theorem's chosen witness is exactly $P[u\otimes u]$, with the
stated global integrability and (S3) identity.

For almost every $t$, the hypothesis gives
$u(\cdot,t)\in L^3(\R^3)$. The slice estimate in
Definition~\ref{def:riesz-pressure}, applied with $r=3/2$, yields
\[
 \norm{p_*(\cdot,t)}_{L^{3/2}}
 \le C_{3/2}\sum_{i,j=1}^3
                    \norm{u_i(\cdot,t)u_j(\cdot,t)}_{L^{3/2}}
 \le 9C_{3/2}\norm{u(\cdot,t)}_{L^3}^2.
\]
Taking the essential supremum proves the estimate.
\end{proof}


\section{The Leray-regularized equations}\label{sec:regularised}

The mollified velocity in the transport term suppresses the derivatives that
would otherwise fall on the advecting field. This gives a local contraction
in the energy norm; the same divergence-free transport cancellation then
prevents higher spatial derivatives from growing faster than a linear
Gronwall bound. We use the Fourier transform on $\R^3$ with phase
$e^{-2\pi i x\cdot\xi}$. In this convention the heat multiplier is
$e^{-4\pi^2t|\xi|^2}$, and the derivative of the heat flow followed by the
Leray projection has an integrable $(t-s)^{-1/2}$ bound in the mild formula.

Fix a radial function $\rho\in C_c^\infty(B_1)$ such that $\rho\ge0$ and
$\int_{\R^3}\rho=1$. For $\varepsilon>0$ set
$\rho_\varepsilon(x)=\varepsilon^{-3}\rho(x/\varepsilon)$ and, componentwise,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-mollifier}
  (\Jeps f)(x)=\int_{\R^3}\rho_\varepsilon(x-y)f(y)\,dy\,.
\end{equation}
We use the pressure $P[F]$ of Definition~\ref{def:riesz-pressure}; its
normalization is $-\Delta P[F]=\sum_{i,j}\partial_i\partial_jF_{ij}$.

\paragraph{Fourier multipliers.}
For a Schwartz function $f$ write
$\widehat f(\xi)=\int_{\R^3}e^{-2\pi i x\cdot\xi}f(x)\,dx$ and extend this
transform to $L^2$ by Plancherel. Vector and tensor transforms are taken
componentwise. Thus $\widehat{\partial_j f}=2\pi i\xi_j\widehat f$ and the
heat semigroup $S(t)=e^{t\Delta}$ has symbol $e^{-4\pi^2t|\xi|^2}$. For
$\xi\ne0$ let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-leray-symbol}
  \Pi(\xi)=I-\frac{\xi\otimes\xi}{|\xi|^2},
\end{equation}
and set $\Pi(0)=I$. This is the orthogonal projection onto $\xi^\perp$.
For a tensor $F=(F_{ij})$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-stokes-multiplier}
  \widehat{K(t)F}(\xi)=e^{-4\pi^2t|\xi|^2}\Pi(\xi)
    \left(2\pi i\sum_{i=1}^3\xi_i\widehat F_{ij}(\xi)\right)_{j=1}^3,
\end{equation}
where the vector in parentheses has components indexed by $j$. For
$\xi\ne0$ the pressure multiplier is
$\widehat{\Riesz{i}\Riesz{j}g}(\xi)=-\xi_i\xi_j|\xi|^{-2}\widehat g(\xi)$.
The value assigned at $\xi=0$ does not affect any of these $L^2$ operators.

\begin{lemma}[Multiplier bounds]\label{lem:reg-multiplier-bounds}
For $t>0$, $v\in L^2(\R^3;\R^3)$ and $F\in L^2(\R^3;\R^{3\times3})$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-multiplier-bounds}
  \norm{S(t)v}_2\le\norm v_2,\qquad
  \norm{K(t)F}_2\le\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\mathrm e\,t}}\norm F_2,\qquad
  \norm{P[F]}_2\le 3\norm F_2\,.
\end{equation}
Moreover $S(t)$ and $\Pi$ preserve weak divergence freedom, and
$\dv K(t)F=0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Plancherel gives the first estimate because $|e^{-4\pi^2t|\xi|^2}|\le1$.
Since $\Pi(\xi)$ has operator norm at most one and
$|F(\xi)^T\xi|\le |F(\xi)|_{\mathrm F}|\xi|$, the norm of the symbol in
\eqref{eq:reg-stokes-multiplier} is at most
$2\pi|\xi|e^{-4\pi^2t|\xi|^2}$. Its maximum over $|\xi|\ge0$ is
$(2\mathrm e t)^{-1/2}$, proving the second estimate. Each scalar double
Riesz multiplier has modulus at most one, so Plancherel gives
$\norm{\Riesz{i}\Riesz{j}F_{ij}}_2\le\norm{F_{ij}}_2$. Summing the nine
terms and applying Cauchy--Schwarz gives the last estimate. The identities
$\xi\cdot\Pi(\xi)z=0$ and $\Pi(\xi)^2=\Pi(\xi)$ give the divergence
claims. The distributional Fourier identity extends from Schwartz fields
to the stated spaces by density and the displayed bounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mollifier bounds]\label{lem:reg-mollifier-bounds}
For every scalar or vector field $f\in L^2(\R^3)$ and every multi-index
$\alpha$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-mollifier-bounds}
  \norm{\partial^\alpha\Jeps f}_{L^\infty}
  \le \varepsilon^{-3/2-|\alpha|}\norm{\partial^\alpha\rho}_{L^2}\norm f_2,
  \qquad \norm{\Jeps f}_2\le\norm f_2\,.
\end{equation}
Convolution commutes with weak spatial derivatives and preserves weak
divergence freedom.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For smooth compactly supported $f$, differentiation under the integral in
\eqref{eq:reg-mollifier} and Cauchy--Schwarz yield
$|\partial^\alpha\Jeps f(x)|\le\norm{\partial^\alpha\rho_\varepsilon}_2\norm f_2$.
The change of variables $y=\varepsilon z$ gives
$\norm{\partial^\alpha\rho_\varepsilon}_2
=\varepsilon^{-3/2-|\alpha|}\norm{\partial^\alpha\rho}_2$. Density of
smooth compactly supported functions in $L^2$ extends the pointwise
convolution formula and the uniform bound to every $f\in L^2$; the same
argument applies to each weak derivative. Young's inequality and
$\norm{\rho_\varepsilon}_1=1$ give the $L^2$ contraction. Since radiality
implies $\rho_\varepsilon(x)=\rho_\varepsilon(-x)$, Fubini's theorem gives
$\int (\Jeps f)\cdot\nabla\psi=\int f\cdot\Jeps(\nabla\psi)$ for
$\psi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$. The right side vanishes if $f$ is weakly
divergence free. All Fubini integrals are absolutely convergent by
Cauchy--Schwarz and compact support of the test and mollifier. This proves
the divergence claim; the weak-derivative commutation follows by applying
the same identity to derivatives of the test function.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The local mild solution.}
For $v:[0,T]\to\J$ define
$F(v)_{ij}(s)=(\Jeps v(s))_i v_j(s)$. The divergence-free condition gives
$\sum_i\partial_iF(v)_{ij}=((\Jeps v)\cdot\nabla)v_j$ in distributions.
The mild equation for the regularized system is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-mild}
  u(t)=S(t)b-\int_0^tK(t-s)F(u)(s)\,ds\,.
\end{equation}
where $b\in\J$ is the initial state.
The integral is a Bochner integral in $L^2$; only its pointwise regular
representative will be used in the theorem below.

\begin{lemma}[Local mild solution]\label{lem:reg-local-mild}
For $b\in\J$ and $\varepsilon>0$, set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-lifespan}
  M=1+\norm b_2,\qquad
  A_\varepsilon=\frac{\norm\rho_2}{\sqrt{2\mathrm e}}\varepsilon^{-3/2},
  \qquad T_0=(16A_\varepsilon M)^{-2}\,.
\end{equation}
There is a unique $u\in C([0,T_0];\J)$ satisfying
\eqref{eq:reg-mild}. Uniqueness holds among all continuous $L^2$ mild
solutions on this interval, and $u(0)=b$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The space $C([0,T];\J)$ with the supremum norm is complete because $\J$ is
closed in $L^2$. For $v,w\in\J$, Lemma~\ref{lem:reg-mollifier-bounds}
gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-tensor-estimates}
  \norm{F(v)}_2\le\norm\rho_2\varepsilon^{-3/2}\norm v_2^2,\qquad
  \norm{F(v)-F(w)}_2\le\norm\rho_2\varepsilon^{-3/2}
    (\norm v_2+\norm w_2)\norm{v-w}_2\,.
\end{equation}
Here the tensor norm is the $L^2$ norm of its Frobenius norm. The second
bound follows from
$F(v)-F(w)=\Jeps(v-w)\otimes v+\Jeps w\otimes(v-w)$ and Hölder's
inequality in space. Lemma~\ref{lem:reg-multiplier-bounds} therefore shows
that for continuous $F:[0,T]\to L^2$, the integrand in
\eqref{eq:reg-mild} is strongly measurable and has norm at most
$(2\mathrm e)^{-1/2}(t-s)^{-1/2}\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\norm{F(r)}_2$. Since
$\int_0^t(t-s)^{-1/2}ds=2\sqrt t$, it is Bochner integrable. Its integral
is continuous in $t$: on $t-s<\eta$ its norm is at most
$2(2\mathrm e)^{-1/2}\sqrt\eta\sup_r\norm{F(r)}_2$; on the complement,
strong continuity of $K(\tau)$ for $\tau>0$, which follows from its Fourier
symbol, and dominated convergence apply. Letting
$\eta\downarrow0$ proves continuity, including at $t=0$.

Let $\Phi$ denote the right side of \eqref{eq:reg-mild} and take the closed
ball of radius $2M$ in $C([0,T_0];\J)$. The first estimate in
\eqref{eq:reg-tensor-estimates} gives
$\norm{\Phi(v)-S(\cdot)b}_{C_tL^2}\le2A_\varepsilon(2M)^2\sqrt{T_0}=M/2$.
Also $\norm{S(t)b}_2\le\norm b_2<M$, so $\Phi$ maps the ball into
itself. The second estimate gives
$\norm{\Phi(v)-\Phi(w)}_{C_tL^2}\le
8A_\varepsilon M\sqrt{T_0}\norm{v-w}_{C_tL^2}=\frac12\norm{v-w}_{C_tL^2}$.
The contraction theorem supplies a fixed point. The map takes values in
$\J$ because both the heat flow and $K(t)$ have divergence-free range.

If $u,v$ are any two continuous $L^2$ solutions of
\eqref{eq:reg-mild}, their difference $d(t)=\norm{u(t)-v(t)}_2$ satisfies
$d(t)\le A_\varepsilon(\norm u_{C_tL^2}+\norm v_{C_tL^2})
\int_0^t(t-s)^{-1/2}d(s)\,ds$. Partition $[0,T_0]$ into intervals of
length $h$ such that
$2A_\varepsilon(\norm u_{C_tL^2}+\norm v_{C_tL^2})\sqrt h<1$. On the
first interval this inequality forces $d=0$ by taking its supremum. On
each next interval the earlier difference is zero, and the same estimate
applies after translating the lower time endpoint. Induction over the
finite partition proves uniqueness.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Global regularity.}
The theorem's initial state is $b_0=\Jeps a$. It belongs to $\J$: if
$a_k\in C_c^\infty(\R^3;\R^3)$ are divergence-free with $a_k\to a$ in
$L^2$, then each $\Jeps a_k$ is smooth, compactly supported and
divergence-free, since derivatives commute with convolution, and
$\norm{\Jeps a_k-\Jeps a}_2\le\norm{a_k-a}_2\to0$ because $\rho\ge0$ and
$\int\rho=1$. It also belongs to every integer Sobolev space. More
precisely,
Lemma~\ref{lem:reg-mollifier-bounds} gives
$\norm{\partial^\alpha\Jeps a}_{L^2}\le
\norm{\partial^\alpha\rho_\varepsilon}_1\norm a_2$ for every $\alpha$;
the derivative kernel is integrable because $\rho$ is smooth and compactly
supported. For each integer $k\ge0$, give $H^k$ the norm
$\norm v_{H^k}^2=\sum_{|\alpha|\le k}\norm{\partial^\alpha v}_2^2$.
The product rule and Lemma~\ref{lem:reg-mollifier-bounds} imply, for a
constant $C_{\varepsilon,k}$ depending only on $\varepsilon,k,\rho$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-hk-product}
  \norm{F(v)}_{H^k}\le C_{\varepsilon,k}\norm v_2\norm v_{H^k}\,.
\end{equation}
Indeed each derivative is a finite sum
$\partial^\alpha F(v)_{ij}=\sum_{\beta\le\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}
(\partial^\beta\Jeps v)_i\partial^{\alpha-\beta}v_j$, and the first
factor is bounded in $L^\infty$ by
$\norm{\partial^\beta\rho_\varepsilon}_2\norm v_2$.

For any $b\in H^k(\R^3;\R^3)\cap\J$, spatial derivatives commute with
$S(t)$ and $K(t)$, and the multiplier
bound in Lemma~\ref{lem:reg-multiplier-bounds} holds with the $H^k$ norms
on both sides. Thus the same mild map is locally contractive in
$C([0,T_k];H^k)$ on a ball whose radius is twice
$1+\norm b_{H^k}$, for a positive time $T_k$ depending only on
$\varepsilon,k,\rho,\norm b_2$ and $\norm b_{H^k}$. The difference
estimate follows by applying the product rule to
$\Jeps(v-w)\otimes v+\Jeps w\otimes(v-w)$; on that ball each term is
bounded in $H^k$ by a constant times the ball radius times
$\norm{v-w}_{H^k}$. The Abel integral contributes the factor $2\sqrt{T_k}$,
so choosing $T_k$ small enough gives both invariance and contraction. The
result agrees with the $L^2$ solution by the uniqueness just proved.

For this local interval, the construction gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-hk-energy}
  u\in C([0,T_k];H^k(\R^3;\R^3))\,.
\end{equation}

This local $H^k$ construction supplies the spatial regularity used to
identify the equation on its interval of existence. After the global $L^2$
energy bound is established, uniform Bessel-potential restarts will give the
higher-regularity path on every finite interval.

\paragraph{The pointwise representative and pressure.}
Fix the local interval $[0,T_4]$ given by the preceding $H^4$ construction.
If $s>m+3/2$, Cauchy--Schwarz gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-fourier-l1}
  \int_{\R^3}|\xi|^m|\widehat f(\xi)|\,d\xi
  \le\left(\int_{\R^3}(1+|\xi|^2)^s|\widehat f(\xi)|^2d\xi\right)^{1/2}
     \left(\int_{\R^3}|\xi|^{2m}(1+|\xi|^2)^{-s}d\xi\right)^{1/2}<\infty.
\end{equation}
The last integral is finite exactly when $s>m+3/2$. For $f\in H^s$, the
inverse Fourier integral of its $L^1$ Fourier representative defines a bounded
continuous function. Testing against Schwartz functions identifies it with
$f$ as a tempered distribution, hence almost everywhere. Applying the same
estimate to derivatives of order at most $m$ permits differentiation under
the inverse integral by dominated convergence. In particular, $H^4(\R^3)$
embeds into $C_b^2(\R^3)$.

For each $t\in[0,T_4]$, set $p(t)=P[F(u(t))]$. The double Riesz multipliers
are bounded on $H^4$ and commute with spatial derivatives. The path $u\in C([0,T_4];H^4)$ and continuity of $J_\varepsilon$ from $L^2$
into each $W^{m,\infty}$ imply that the tensor $F(u(t))$ and the canonical
pressure $p(t)$ depend continuously on $t$ in $H^4$. The slice formula agrees almost everywhere in
time with the jointly measurable pressure of Definition~
\ref{def:riesz-pressure}, and fixes the Riesz representative at every time.
Fourier inversion gives continuous spatial derivatives of $u$ through order
two and of $p$ through order one, jointly with time on this local interval.
These fields are uniformly bounded there. The formula for $\partial_tu$ has right side
$\Delta u-(J_\varepsilon u\cdot\nabla)u-\nabla p$, which is continuous
and bounded on compact time strips; the same formula gives a
continuous spatial $L^2$ path for $\partial_tu$.

Let $\mathcal L F$ have Fourier multiplier
$\Pi(\xi)(2\pi i\xi_i\widehat F_{ij})_j$. The mild equation gives
$\partial_tu-\Delta u+\mathcal L F(u)=0$ in distributions. The Fourier
symbols give $\mathcal L F=\dv F+\nabla p$: the gradient part of $\dv F$
has symbol $(I-\Pi)(2\pi i\xi_i\widehat F_{ij})_j$, while $\nabla p$ has
its negative by the multiplier $-\xi_i\xi_j|\xi|^{-2}$. Thus, for every compactly supported test field $\varphi$, the pairing
$G_\varphi(t)=\int u(x,t)\cdot\varphi(x)\,dx$ has distributional derivative
\begin{equation*}
  G_\varphi'(t)=\int u(x,t)\cdot\Delta\varphi(x)\,dx
   +\int u_i(x,t)(J_\varepsilon u(x,t))_j\partial_j\varphi_i(x)\,dx
   +\int p(x,t)\,\dv\varphi(x)\,dx\,.
\end{equation*}
The right side is continuous in $t$. The one-dimensional fundamental
theorem of calculus for weak derivatives gives, for $0<s<t<T_4$,
\begin{equation*}
  G_\varphi(t)-G_\varphi(s)=\int_s^tG_\varphi'(\tau)\,d\tau\,.
\end{equation*}
Since this holds for every compactly supported spatial test and all fields in
the identity are jointly continuous, test separation gives, for each $x$ and
$0<s<t<T_4$,
\begin{equation*}
  u(x,t)-u(x,s)=\int_s^t\bigl(\Delta u-(J_\varepsilon u\cdot\nabla)u
                  -\nabla p\bigr)(x,\tau)\,d\tau\,.
\end{equation*}
Differentiating this identity in time shows that $\partial_tu$ exists and is
jointly continuous. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-classical-equation}
  \partial_tu-\Delta u+\dv F(u)+\nabla p=0\,.
\end{equation}
This is the pointwise equation on the local interval. Since
$\dv(J_\varepsilon u)=0$,
$\dv F(u)=((J_\varepsilon u)\cdot\nabla)u$.

\begin{theorem}[Global Leray-regularized solution]\label{thm:regularised}
Let $a\in\J$ and $\varepsilon>0$. There are
$u_\varepsilon\colon\R^3\times[0,\infty)\to\R^3$ and
$p_\varepsilon\colon\R^3\times(0,\infty)\to\R$ with the following
properties.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(R\arabic*)]
\item The map $t\mapsto u_\varepsilon(\cdot,t)$ is continuous from
  $[0,\infty)$ to $L^2(\R^3)$, $u_\varepsilon(\cdot,0)=\Jeps a$, and
  $u_\varepsilon(\cdot,t)$ is weakly divergence free for every $t\ge0$
  (hence classically divergence free for $t>0$).
\item On $\R^3\times(0,\infty)$, $u_\varepsilon$, its first and second
  spatial derivatives, its time derivative, $p_\varepsilon$, and the first
  spatial derivatives of $p_\varepsilon$ are continuous. For every
  $0<\delta<T<\infty$ these fields are bounded on
  $\R^3\times[\delta,T]$, and
  $u_\varepsilon,\nabla u_\varepsilon,\nabla^2u_\varepsilon,
  \partial_tu_\varepsilon\in L^2(\R^3\times(\delta,T))$ and
  $p_\varepsilon\in L^2(\R^3\times(\delta,T))$.
\item The pointwise equation
  $\partial_tu_\varepsilon-\Delta u_\varepsilon+
  ((\Jeps u_\varepsilon)\cdot\nabla)u_\varepsilon+
  \nabla p_\varepsilon=0$ holds on $\R^3\times(0,\infty)$.
\item For every $t>0$,
  $p_\varepsilon(\cdot,t)=
  P[(\Jeps u_\varepsilon)_i u_{\varepsilon,j}](\cdot,t)$, with the
  sum over $i,j$ understood and the sign convention of
  Definition~\ref{def:riesz-pressure}; in particular,
  $-\Delta p_\varepsilon=\sum_{i,j}\partial_i\partial_j
  ((\Jeps u_\varepsilon)_i u_{\varepsilon,j})$.
\item For every $t\ge0$,
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-energy-equality}
    \norm{u_\varepsilon(t)}_2^2+2\int_0^t
      \norm{\nabla u_\varepsilon(s)}_2^2\,ds
      =\norm{\Jeps a}_2^2\,.
  \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
The solution is unique among continuous $L^2$ solutions of the projected
mild equation \eqref{eq:reg-mild}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Lemma~\ref{lem:reg-local-mild} gives the unique local mild solution when
applied with initial state $b_0=\Jeps a$. The Sobolev construction above
gives, on its interval of existence, a solution
in every $H^k$; the projected equation and the Riesz pressure give the
classical equation \eqref{eq:reg-classical-equation} for positive time.
We first establish its energy equality on a positive-time slab.

For $0<\delta<t$, multiply \eqref{eq:reg-classical-equation} by $2u$ and
integrate over space with a smooth cutoff $\chi_R$ satisfying
$0\le\chi_R\le1$, equal to one in $B_R$, zero outside $B_{2R}$, and
$|\nabla\chi_R|\le C/R$. The
identities $u\cdot\partial_tu=\frac12\partial_t|u|^2$,
$-2u\cdot\Delta u=-\Delta|u|^2+2|\nabla u|^2$, and
$2u\cdot((\Jeps u)\cdot\nabla)u=\dv(|u|^2\Jeps u)$ give the localized
balance. The pressure term is $2\dv(pu)$ because $\dv u=0$. After
integration in time, every cutoff error is bounded by a constant times
$R^{-1}$ times one of
$\norm{u}_{L^2((\delta,t);L^2)}\norm{\nabla u}_{L^2((\delta,t);L^2)}$,
$\norm{\Jeps u}_{L^\infty((\delta,t)\times\R^3)}
\norm{u}_{L^2((\delta,t);L^2)}^2$, or
$\norm p_{L^2((\delta,t);L^2)}\norm u_{L^2((\delta,t);L^2)}$.
These quantities are finite by the Sobolev bounds and the pressure
multiplier estimate. Thus the errors tend to zero as $R\to\infty$.
Dominated convergence for the endpoint norms and for the dissipation gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-energy-positive}
  \norm{u(t)}_2^2+2\int_\delta^t\norm{\nabla u(s)}_2^2\,ds
  =\norm{u(\delta)}_2^2\,.
\end{equation}
As $\delta\downarrow0$, continuity in $L^2$ gives
$\norm{u(\delta)}_2\to\norm{\Jeps a}_2$. The integrals on the left
increase to the integral over $(0,t)$, which is finite by
\eqref{eq:reg-energy-positive}. This proves
\eqref{eq:reg-energy-equality} on the local interval, including t=0.

In particular $\norm{u(t)}_2\le\norm{\Jeps a}_2\le\norm a_2$. The
lifespan in Lemma~\ref{lem:reg-local-mild}, applied at any later time to the
value $u(t)$, is bounded below by
$(16A_\varepsilon(1+\norm a_2))^{-2}$, independently of $t$. Repeating the
local construction therefore gives a global mild solution, and uniqueness
identifies the successive pieces. The positive-time energy argument on each
piece gives \eqref{eq:reg-energy-equality} for every $t$.

For each $k$ and finite $T$, apply the Bessel-potential mild estimates of
order $2k$. The local continuation step depends on the $L^2$ bound from
(R5), not on the current $H^{2k}$ norm. That bound is uniform at every restart
time, so one positive step length covers $[0,T]$ with finitely many pieces.
Mild uniqueness identifies them with the global $L^2$ solution. The resulting
continuous Bessel path and the bounded coordinate-derivative maps into $L^2$
give $u\in C([0,T];H^k)$ for every $k$. This gives the asserted higher regularity globally.

The Fourier embedding, canonical pressure construction, and weak-to-classical
fundamental-theorem argument above now apply on every finite interval. They
give the regularity clauses and the interval pointwise equation (R3) for all
positive times, together with the every-time pressure identity (R4). The
paths $u$, $\nabla u$, $\nabla^2u$, $p$, and $\nabla p$ are continuous
in their spatial $L^2$ norms on compact positive-time intervals. The formula
for $\partial_tu$ gives the same continuity for its spatial $L^2$ norm.
These bounds yield the required space-time $L^2$ clauses by integration in
time.
This proves (R1)--(R5) and the stated uniqueness.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The regularized pressure in $L^2$]\label{lem:reg-pressure-L2}
For every $t>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-pressure-L2}
  \norm{p_\varepsilon(t)}_2\le C\norm{\Jeps u_\varepsilon(t)}_4
    \norm{u_\varepsilon(t)}_4\,,
\end{equation}
where C is an absolute constant.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
At each positive time the theorem gives $u_\varepsilon(t)\in H^1(\R^3)$.
For completeness, the Sobolev bound used here follows first for
$v\in C_c^\infty(\R^3;\R^3)$ from the coordinate-line estimate
$|g(x)|\le\int_\R|\partial_i g(x_1,\ldots,s,\ldots,x_3)|ds$ applied to
$g=|v|^4$. To see the resulting $W^{1,1}$ inequality explicitly, put
$G_i(x_{\widehat i})=\int_\R|\partial_i g(x_1,\ldots,s,\ldots,x_3)|ds$,
where $x_{\widehat i}$ denotes the two coordinates other than $x_i$.
Then $|g|^{3/2}\le(G_1G_2G_3)^{1/2}$. Integrating first in $x_1$ and
using Cauchy--Schwarz bounds the integral by
\begin{equation*}
\int_{\R^2}G_1(x_2,x_3)^{1/2}
  \left(\int_\R G_2(x_1,x_3)dx_1\right)^{1/2}
  \left(\int_\R G_3(x_1,x_2)dx_1\right)^{1/2}dx_2dx_3\,.
\end{equation*}
A second Cauchy--Schwarz inequality in $(x_2,x_3)$ bounds this by
$\prod_{i=1}^3(\int_{\R^2}G_i)^{1/2}$. Since
$\int G_i=\norm{\partial_i g}_1$, taking the power $2/3$ gives
$\norm g_{3/2}\le\prod_i\norm{\partial_i g}_1^{1/3}
\le C\norm{\nabla g}_1$. Since
$|\nabla(|v|^4)|\le4|v|^3|\nabla v|$, Hölder yields
$\norm v_6^4\le C\norm v_6^3\norm{\nabla v}_2$, hence
$\norm v_6\le C\norm{\nabla v}_2$. Approximation in $H^1$ extends this
inequality to every $H^1$ field. Interpolation gives
$\norm v_4\le\norm v_2^{1/4}\norm v_6^{3/4}$, so both $u_\varepsilon(t)$
and $\Jeps u_\varepsilon(t)$ belong to $L^4$. Indeed, Young's inequality
and commutation with weak derivatives give
$\norm{\Jeps u_\varepsilon(t)}_2+
\norm{\nabla\Jeps u_\varepsilon(t)}_2
\le\norm{u_\varepsilon(t)}_2+\norm{\nabla u_\varepsilon(t)}_2$,
so the same Sobolev and interpolation bounds apply.

Set $F_{ij}=(\Jeps u_\varepsilon)_i u_{\varepsilon,j}$. Its Frobenius
norm is $|F|_{\mathrm F}=|\Jeps u_\varepsilon||u_\varepsilon|$, and
Hölder gives $\norm F_2\le\norm{\Jeps u_\varepsilon}_4
\norm{u_\varepsilon}_4$. The p=2 Riesz bound in
Definition~\ref{def:riesz-pressure}, or equivalently
Lemma~\ref{lem:reg-multiplier-bounds}, now proves
\eqref{eq:reg-pressure-L2} with an absolute constant in dimension three.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Local energy identity]\label{lem:reg-local-energy}
For every $\psi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3\times(0,\infty))$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-local-energy}
  2\iint |\nabla u_\varepsilon|^2\psi
  =\iint |u_\varepsilon|^2(\partial_t\psi+\Delta\psi)
   +\bigl(|u_\varepsilon|^2\Jeps u_\varepsilon
        +2p_\varepsilon u_\varepsilon\bigr)\cdot\nabla\psi\,.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Multiply the pointwise equation in (R3) by $2u_\varepsilon\psi$ and
integrate over the compact support of $\psi$. The time term is
$\partial_t|u_\varepsilon|^2\psi$; integration by parts in time gives
$-|u_\varepsilon|^2\partial_t\psi$. The Laplacian term is
$-\Delta|u_\varepsilon|^2\psi+2|\nabla u_\varepsilon|^2\psi$; moving
$\Delta|u_\varepsilon|^2$ onto $\psi$ gives the displayed $\Delta\psi$
term. Since $\dv(\Jeps u_\varepsilon)=0$,
$2u_\varepsilon\cdot((\Jeps u_\varepsilon)\cdot\nabla)u_\varepsilon
=\dv(|u_\varepsilon|^2\Jeps u_\varepsilon)$. Since
$\dv u_\varepsilon=0$,
$2u_\varepsilon\cdot\nabla p_\varepsilon=2\dv(p_\varepsilon u_\varepsilon)$.
Spatial integration by parts moves both divergences onto $\psi$ with a
negative sign; the compact support eliminates boundary terms. Rearranging
gives \eqref{eq:reg-local-energy}. All integrations are justified by the
continuity in (R2) on the compact support.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Weak momentum identity]\label{lem:reg-momentum}
For every $\varphi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3\times(0,\infty);\R^3)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-momentum}
  \iint\left[-u_\varepsilon\cdot\partial_t\varphi
  -(\Jeps u_\varepsilon)_j u_{\varepsilon,i}\partial_j\varphi_i
  +\partial_j u_{\varepsilon,i}\partial_j\varphi_i
  -p_\varepsilon\dv\varphi\right]=0\,.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Integrate the pointwise equation (R3) against $\varphi$. Integration by
parts in time gives the first term of \eqref{eq:reg-momentum}. For
diffusion, one spatial integration by parts gives
$\int\partial_j u_{\varepsilon,i}\partial_j\varphi_i$. For convection,
write it as $\partial_j((\Jeps u_\varepsilon)_j u_{\varepsilon,i})$;
this is valid because $\dv(\Jeps u_\varepsilon)=0$. Integration by parts
gives the negative transport term displayed. The pressure term gives
$-p_\varepsilon\dv\varphi$. Each test is compactly supported in the open
space-time cylinder, so no spatial or temporal boundary term remains. The
regularity (R2) makes every integral finite. The resulting identity is
(S3) of Definition~\ref{def:sws} with the advecting factor
$(\Jeps u_\varepsilon)_j$ and zero force.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Departure from \cite{OzanskiPooley2018}]
The regularized existence proof in~\cite[Theorem~4.2, p.~41]{OzanskiPooley2018}
uses heat and Oseen kernels. Here the local construction uses the $L^2$ Fourier
multiplier \eqref{eq:reg-stokes-multiplier}; Plancherel gives the
$(t-s)^{-1/2}$ estimate directly. The weak identity is derived from the
pointwise equation rather than copied from the source: its Definition~4.1 (printed p.~40)
display has a sign and tensor-slot mismatch with the regularized equation.
The Riesz formula fixes the canonical time-slice pressure,
and the lifespan in \eqref{eq:reg-lifespan} also covers zero initial data.
These changes affect the proof, not the properties (R1)--(R5).
\end{remark}

\section{Uniform bounds}\label{sec:uniform}

% Uniform estimates for the regularized solutions.

For the solutions of Theorem~\ref{thm:regularised}, energy equality (R5) gives
the following separate global bounds.
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:reg-energy}
For every $0<\varepsilon\leq1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-energy-consequences}
\begin{aligned}
  \sup_{t\geq0}\norm{u_\varepsilon(t)}_2^2&\leq\norm{a}_2^2,\\
  2\int_0^\infty\norm{\nabla u_\varepsilon(s)}_2^2\,ds
       &\leq\norm{a}_2^2\,.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Moreover, spatial convolution is contractive in $L^2$: for every $t\geq0$,
$\norm{\Jeps u_\varepsilon(t)}_2\leq\norm{u_\varepsilon(t)}_2$, and also
$\norm{\Jeps a}_2\leq\norm{a}_2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For every finite $T\geq0$, (R5) and $L^2$ contraction give
\begin{equation*}
  \norm{u_\varepsilon(T)}_2^2
  +2\int_0^T\norm{\nabla u_\varepsilon(s)}_2^2\,ds
  =\norm{\Jeps a}_2^2\leq\norm{a}_2^2\,.
\end{equation*}
Each term on the left is nonnegative, so the first bound follows for every
$T$, and monotone convergence as $T\to\infty$ gives the second. Young's
inequality for spatial convolution gives both stated contraction bounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:reg-ten-thirds}
For every $T\in(0,\infty]$ and every $0<\varepsilon\leq1$,
\[
  \norm{u_\varepsilon}_{L^{10/3}(\R^3\times(0,T))}
  +\norm{\Jeps u_\varepsilon}_{L^{10/3}(\R^3\times(0,T))}
  \leq C\norm{a}_2,
\]
where $C$ depends only on the dimension and the interpolation constant in
Lemma~\ref{lem:u-ten-thirds}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By (R1), $u_\varepsilon(\cdot,t)\in L^2(\R^3)$ for every $t\geq0$. By (R2),
each positive-time slice is smooth in space and its classical gradient is its
weak gradient. The bound in (R5) gives
$\nabla u_\varepsilon\in L^2(\R^3\times(0,\infty))$, so Fubini implies
$\nabla u_\varepsilon(\cdot,t)\in L^2(\R^3)$ for almost every $t>0$. Thus
$u_\varepsilon(\cdot,t)\in H^1(\R^3)$ for almost every $t>0$, and the spatial
interpolation estimate of Lemma~\ref{lem:u-ten-thirds} gives, for those times,
\[
  \int_{\R^3}|u_\varepsilon(x,t)|^{10/3}\,dx
  \leq C\norm{u_\varepsilon(t)}_2^{4/3}
          \norm{\nabla u_\varepsilon(t)}_2^2.
\]
Integrating in time and using \eqref{eq:reg-energy-consequences} proves the
bound for $u_\varepsilon$, with a constant independent of $T$. Since
$\rho_\varepsilon$ is nonnegative and has integral one, Young's convolution
inequality gives
\[
  \norm{\Jeps u_\varepsilon(\cdot,t)}_{10/3}
  \leq \norm{u_\varepsilon(\cdot,t)}_{10/3}
\]
for almost every $t$, which proves the other bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:reg-pressure-bound}
Let $p_\varepsilon$ be the pressure in (R4). For every $0<\varepsilon\leq1$,
\[
  \norm{p_\varepsilon}_{L^{5/3}(\R^3\times(0,\infty))}
       \leq C\norm{a}_2^2.
\]
Moreover, for every $T>0$,
\[
  \norm{p_\varepsilon}_{L^{3/2}(\R^3\times(0,T))}
       \leq C\norm{a}_2^2 T^{1/6}.
\]
The constants depend only on the dimension, the interpolation constant in
Lemma~\ref{lem:reg-ten-thirds}, and the Calder\'on--Zygmund operator bounds at
exponents $5/3$ and $3/2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Set $F_\varepsilon=(\Jeps u_\varepsilon)\otimes u_\varepsilon$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:reg-ten-thirds} and H\"older's inequality,
\[
  \norm{F_\varepsilon}_{L^{5/3}(\R^3\times(0,\infty))}
  \leq \norm{\Jeps u_\varepsilon}_{L^{10/3}}
        \norm{u_\varepsilon}_{L^{10/3}}
  \leq C\norm{a}_2^2.
\]
The Calder\'on--Zygmund bound in Definition~\ref{def:riesz-pressure}, applied
at exponent $5/3$, and (R4) imply the first assertion. For the second, spatial
H\"older and \eqref{eq:reg-energy-consequences} give
\[
  \norm{F_\varepsilon}_{L^1(\R^3\times(0,T))}
  \leq \int_0^T\norm{\Jeps u_\varepsilon(t)}_2
                     \norm{u_\varepsilon(t)}_2\,dt
  \leq T\norm{a}_2^2.
\]
Interpolation on $\R^3\times(0,T)$ yields
\[
  \norm{F_\varepsilon}_{L^{3/2}}
  \leq \norm{F_\varepsilon}_{L^1}^{1/6}
       \norm{F_\varepsilon}_{L^{5/3}}^{5/6}
  \leq C\norm{a}_2^2T^{1/6}.
\]
Applying the same pressure bound at exponent $3/2$ proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:reg-tails}
There is a constant $C$, independent of $a$, $\varepsilon$, $R_1$, $R_2$ and
$t$, such that for $0<\varepsilon\leq1$, $0<R_1<R_2$ and $t\geq0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reg-tails}
  \int_{|x|>R_2}|u_\varepsilon(x,t)|^2\,dx
  \leq \int_{|x|>R_1}|\Jeps a(x)|^2\,dx
    +\frac{C\bigl(\norm{a}_2^2t^{1/2}
                   +\norm{a}_2^3t^{1/4}\bigr)}{R_2-R_1}.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
  \int_{|x|>R_1}|\Jeps a(x)|^2\,dx
  \leq \int_{|x|>R_1-1}|a(x)|^2\,dx,
\]
where the right side is understood to be the integral over all of $\R^3$
when $R_1-1<0$. The constant $C$ depends only on the dimension, the fixed
cutoff profile, and the universal Calder\'on--Zygmund and
Gagliardo--Nirenberg constants.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
At $t=0$, (R1) identifies $u_\varepsilon(0)=\Jeps a$, and the inequality
follows from $R_1<R_2$. Thus assume $t>0$ below.
Write $A=\norm{a}_2$ and $D=R_2-R_1$. Choose a smooth function
$\eta\colon\R\to[0,1]$ which is zero on $(-\infty,0]$, one on $[1,\infty)$,
and has bounded derivative. The radial function
\[
  f(x)=\eta\!\left(\frac{|x|-R_1}{D}\right)
\]
is smooth (it is constant near the origin), vanishes on $B_{R_1}$, equals one
outside $B_{R_2}$, and satisfies $|\nabla f|\leq C/D$.

We first justify the localized energy identity without a boundary assumption
at spatial infinity. Choose smooth cutoffs $\chi_N$ with $0\leq\chi_N\leq1$,
equal to one on $B_N$, supported in $B_{2N}$, and
$|\nabla\chi_N|\leq C/N$, and set $q_N=f\chi_N$. For $0<\delta<t$, multiply
(R3) by $2q_Nu_\varepsilon$, sum over the components, and integrate over
$\R^3\times(\delta,t)$. The test field is compactly supported in space.
Integration by parts has no spatial boundary terms; the convection term uses
$\dv(\Jeps u_\varepsilon)=0$, and the pressure term uses
$\dv u_\varepsilon=0$. It gives
\begin{align*}
  &\int q_N|u_\varepsilon(t)|^2
       +2\int_\delta^t\!\int q_N|\nabla u_\varepsilon|^2
   =\int q_N|u_\varepsilon(\delta)|^2\\
  &\quad+\int_\delta^t\!\int\Bigl(
       -2(u_\varepsilon)_i\,\partial_kq_N\,
                           \partial_k(u_\varepsilon)_i
       +|u_\varepsilon|^2(\Jeps u_\varepsilon)_k\partial_kq_N
       +2p_\varepsilon(u_\varepsilon)_i\partial_iq_N\Bigr).
\end{align*}
Here and below, repeated spatial indices are summed from $1$ to $3$.

To pass to $N\to\infty$, the endpoint terms converge by dominated
convergence, since $u_\varepsilon(\cdot,t)$ and
$u_\varepsilon(\cdot,\delta)$ are in $L^2$; the dissipation term converges
by domination by $|\nabla u_\varepsilon|^2$. The terms with $\chi_N\nabla f$
converge by dominated convergence as well. The terms with
$f\nabla\chi_N$ vanish: their absolute values are bounded by $C/N$ times the
respective integrals of $|u_\varepsilon||\nabla u_\varepsilon|$,
$|\Jeps u_\varepsilon||u_\varepsilon|^2$, and
$|p_\varepsilon||u_\varepsilon|$. These are integrable on
$\R^3\times(\delta,t)$. Indeed, (R1) gives
$u_\varepsilon(\cdot,s)\in L^2(\R^3)$ for every $s\geq0$, and (R2) identifies
the classical gradient with the weak gradient on each positive-time slice.
By (R5) and Fubini, $u_\varepsilon(\cdot,s)\in H^1(\R^3)$ for almost every
$s\in(0,t)$. For these times, set
$g(s)=\norm{\nabla u_\varepsilon(s)}_2$. The Gagliardo--Nirenberg inequality
gives the first estimate below. Young's inequality also gives
$\norm{\Jeps u_\varepsilon(s)}_4\leq\norm{u_\varepsilon(s)}_4$, and (R4) with
the Calder\'on--Zygmund bound at exponent $2$ gives the pressure estimate.
\[
  \norm{u_\varepsilon(s)}_4^2
      \leq C\norm{u_\varepsilon(s)}_2^{1/2}g(s)^{3/2},
  \qquad
  \norm{p_\varepsilon(s)}_2
      \leq C\norm{\Jeps u_\varepsilon(s)}_4
                \norm{u_\varepsilon(s)}_4
      \leq C\norm{u_\varepsilon(s)}_4^2.
\]
Thus the second and third integrands are time integrable by
$g\in L^2(0,t)$, and the first is integrable by Cauchy--Schwarz in time.
Letting next $\delta\downarrow0$ is valid: (R1) gives
$u_\varepsilon(\delta)\to\Jeps a$ in $L^2$, the dissipation is integrable by
(R5), and the error terms are integrable down to time zero by the same bounds.
We obtain the identity with $f$ in place of $q_N$ and with initial time zero.

The initial-time error is bounded by
$\int f|\Jeps a|^2\leq\int_{|x|>R_1}|\Jeps a|^2$. To estimate the other
terms, (R5), convolution contraction and Gagliardo--Nirenberg imply
\begin{align*}
  \int_0^t\norm{u_\varepsilon(s)}_2g(s)\,ds
     &\leq C A^2t^{1/2},\\
  \int_0^t\norm{\Jeps u_\varepsilon(s)}_2
                  \norm{u_\varepsilon(s)}_4^2\,ds
     &\leq C A^{3/2}\int_0^t g(s)^{3/2}\,ds
      \leq C A^3t^{1/4},\\
  \int_0^t\norm{p_\varepsilon(s)}_2
                  \norm{u_\varepsilon(s)}_2\,ds
     &\leq C A^{3/2}\int_0^t g(s)^{3/2}\,ds
      \leq C A^3t^{1/4}.
\end{align*}
The first line bounds the term with $\nabla u_\varepsilon\nabla f$; the next
two bound the convection and pressure terms with $\nabla f$. Using
$|\nabla f|\leq C/D$ in the localized identity therefore bounds its total
error by $C(A^2t^{1/2}+A^3t^{1/4})/D$. Since these estimates use only the
common energy bound $\norm{u_\varepsilon(s)}_2\le A$ from (R5) and the
pressure identity (R4), the same dimension, cutoff-profile, and operator
constants work for every datum, every $0<\varepsilon\le1$, and every $t\ge0$.
Since $f=1$ on $\{|x|>R_2\}$, this proves \eqref{eq:reg-tails}.

Finally, Jensen's inequality and the support condition
$\supp\rho_\varepsilon\subset B_\varepsilon$ give
\[
  \int_{|x|>R_1}|\Jeps a(x)|^2\,dx
  \leq\int\rho_\varepsilon(z)
       \int_{|x+z|>R_1}|a(x)|^2\,dx\,dz
  \leq\int_{|x|>R_1-\varepsilon}|a(x)|^2\,dx
  \leq\int_{|x|>R_1-1}|a(x)|^2\,dx.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Repair of Lemma 4.3 in \cite{OzanskiPooley2018}]
The tail estimate is the separation-of-energy estimate of Lemma 4.3
of \cite{OzanskiPooley2018}. In place of its
Newton-potential estimate for the pressure, the proof above uses (R4), the
Calder\'on--Zygmund estimate in $L^2$, and the Gagliardo--Nirenberg inequality.
It also supplies the spatial cutoff limit and the limit to the initial time.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:reg-equicontinuity}
For every $w\in\Cc(\R^3;\R^3)$, every $0<\varepsilon\leq1$, and every
$0\leq s<t<\infty$,
\[
  \left|\int_{\R^3}
      (u_\varepsilon(x,t)-u_\varepsilon(x,s))\cdot w(x)\,dx\right|
  \leq C(A)N(w)|t-s|^{2/5},
\]
where
\[
  N(w)=\norm{w}_2+\norm{\Delta w}_2+
       \norm{\nabla w}_{L^\infty(\R^3)}+
       \norm{\nabla w}_{L^{5/2}(\R^3)},
  \qquad A=\norm{a}_2,\qquad C(A)=C_0(A+A^2).
\]
The constant $C_0$ depends only on the dimension and the fixed
Calder\'on--Zygmund operator bounds.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $w\in\Cc(\R^3;\R^3)$, the weak momentum equation gives the
distributional derivative of $G_w(\tau)=\int u_\varepsilon(x,\tau)\cdot
w(x)\,dx$ on $(0,\infty)$:
\begin{equation*}
  G_w'(\tau)=\int u_\varepsilon\cdot\Delta w
    +\int (u_\varepsilon)_i(J_\varepsilon u_\varepsilon)_j\partial_jw_i
    +\int p_\varepsilon\dv w\,.
\end{equation*}
The integrations by parts have no spatial boundary terms because $w$ is
compactly supported; the convection term uses
$\dv(J_\varepsilon u_\varepsilon)=0$. The first two terms are bounded in
absolute value by $A\norm{\Delta w}_2+A^2\norm{\nabla w}_{L^\infty}$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:reg-pressure-bound} and H\"older's inequality give, for every
$h>0$,
\begin{equation*}
  \int_0^h\left|\int p_\varepsilon\dv w\right|d\tau
   \le C A^2\norm{\dv w}_{L^{5/2}}h^{2/5}\,.
\end{equation*}
Thus $G_w'$ is integrable down to zero. The weak-derivative fundamental
theorem first gives
\begin{equation*}
  G_w(t)-G_w(0)=\int_0^tG_w'(\tau)\,d\tau\,,\qquad t>0\,,
\end{equation*}
where the endpoint value is justified by the $L^2$ continuity in (R1).
Subtracting this identity at $s$ from the identity at $t$ yields the single
increment formula
\begin{equation*}
  G_w(t)-G_w(s)=\int_s^tG_w'(\tau)\,d\tau
  \qquad(0\le s<t)\,.
\end{equation*}
The diffusion and convection terms on $(s,t)$ are bounded by
\begin{equation*}
  A\norm{\Delta w}_2(t-s)
   +A^2\norm{\nabla w}_{L^\infty}(t-s),
\end{equation*}
and the pressure term by
\begin{equation*}
  C A^2\norm{\dv w}_{L^{5/2}}(t-s)^{2/5}
   \le C A^2\norm{\nabla w}_{L^{5/2}}(t-s)^{2/5}.
\end{equation*}
For $t-s\le1$, use $t-s\le(t-s)^{2/5}$. For $t-s\ge1$, (R1) and
\eqref{eq:reg-energy-consequences} give
\begin{equation*}
  |G_w(t)-G_w(s)|\le2A\norm w_2
       \le2A\norm w_2(t-s)^{2/5}.
\end{equation*}
This proves the result with a constant of the stated form, uniformly in
$\varepsilon$.
\end{proof}

\section{Compactness}\label{sec:compactness}

\subsection{A compactness lemma}\label{subsec:compactness}

Spatial mollification separates the compactness argument into fixed-scale convergence and a small gradient error. At a fixed scale, pairings with every translate of the mollifier control the time variable, while the local $L^2$ bound controls spatial variation. The local gradient bound then makes the mollification error vanish in space-time as the scale tends to zero.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:compactness}
Let $U\subset\R^3$ be open and let $I\subset\R$ be an open interval. For each $n\in\N$, let $u_n\colon U\times I\to\R^3$ and $Du_n\colon U\times I\to\R^{3\times3}$ be measurable, with $Du_n(\cdot,t)$ the weak spatial gradient of $u_n(\cdot,t)$ for almost every $t\in I$. Suppose that for every compact $K\subset U$ and compact interval $J\subset I$ there are finite constants $M_{K,J}$ and $G_{K,J}$ such that
\begin{equation*}
\sup_n\sup_{t\in J}\int_K\abs{u_n(x,t)}^2\,dx\le M_{K,J}^2,
\qquad
\sup_n\int_J\int_K\abs{Du_n(x,t)}^2\,dx\,dt\le G_{K,J}^2\,.
\end{equation*}
Suppose also that for every such $K,J$ there are finite constants $A_{K,J},P_{K,J}$ and an exponent $\theta_{K,J}\in(0,1]$, independent of $n,s,t,w$, such that for all $n$, $s,t\in J$, and $w\in\Cc(U;\R^3)$ with $\supp w\subset K$,
\begin{align*}
\abs{\int_U (u_n(x,t)-u_n(x,s))\cdot w(x)\,dx}
&\le A_{K,J}\abs{t-s}\Bigl(\norm{\Delta w}_{L^2(U)}+\norm{\nabla w}_{L^\infty(U)}\Bigr)\\
&\quad+P_{K,J}\abs{t-s}^{\theta_{K,J}}\norm{\dv w}_{L^\infty(U)}\,.
\end{align*}
The constants may depend only on $K,J$ and the uniform bounds and moduli assumed there, not on $n$. Then there is a subsequence $(n_k)$ and a jointly measurable function $u\colon U\times I\to\R^3$, defined at every point, such that:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item for every $t\in I$ and compact $K\subset U$, $u_{n_k}(\cdot,t)\rightharpoonup u(\cdot,t)$ weakly in $L^2(K)$, and for each $t$ the function $u(\cdot,t)$ agrees almost everywhere on $U$ with the resulting local weak limit;
\item $u_{n_k}\to u$ strongly in $L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(U\times I)$;
\item after passing to the same subsequence, $Du_{n_k}\rightharpoonup Du$ weakly in $L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(U\times I)$, where $Du(\cdot,t)$ is the weak spatial gradient of $u(\cdot,t)$ for almost every $t\in I$;
\item for every $w\in L^2(U;\R^3)$ with compact support, the map $t\mapsto\int_Uu(x,t)\cdot w(x)\,dx$ is continuous on $I$;
\item the local bounds pass to the limit: for every compact $K\subset U$ and compact interval $J\subset I$,
\begin{equation*}
\sup_{t\in J}\int_K\abs{u(x,t)}^2\,dx\le M_{K,J}^2,
\qquad
\int_J\int_K\abs{Du(x,t)}^2\,dx\,dt\le G_{K,J}^2\,.
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose a nested countable exhaustion of $U$ by relatively compact open sets $V_j$ and, for each $j$, a countable family in $\Cc(V_j;\R^3)$ dense in $L^2(V_j;\R^3)$. For each field in these families, the pairings with $u_n(\cdot,t)$ are uniformly bounded on compact time intervals by the slice bound on $\overline{V_j}$ and are equicontinuous by the assumed modulus. Arzel\`a--Ascoli and a diagonal subsequence give uniform convergence of these pairings on every compact time interval. At each fixed $t$, their limits extend to bounded linear functionals on each $L^2(V_j;\R^3)$, with norms bounded by the corresponding slice bound on $\overline{V_j}$. The Riesz representation theorem gives compatible limits $u_t\in L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\R^3)$; compatibility follows because the limiting pairings agree on tests supported in intersections of the sets $V_j$. For any compact $K\subset U$ and $f\in L^2(K;\R^3)$, choose $V_j$ containing $K$ and approximate the zero extension of $f$ in $L^2(V_j)$ by the dense family. The uniform slice bound then gives $u_{n_k}(\cdot,t)\rightharpoonup u_t$ in $L^2(K)$ for every $t$. The same uniform approximation shows that $t\mapsto\int_Uu_t\cdot w$ is continuous for every compactly supported $L^2$ test $w$.

Fix a nonnegative smooth bump $\beta$ with inner radius $1/2$ and outer radius $1$, equal to one on $B_{1/2}(0)$ and supported in $B_1(0)$. Normalize it by setting $\rho=\beta/\int_{\R^3}\beta(x)\,dx$, and set $\rho_\delta(x)=\delta^{-3}\rho(x/\delta)$. For each fixed $\delta=1/m$, the mollified fields $u_{n_k}*\rho_\delta$ are uniformly bounded on compact subsets whose $\delta$-neighborhood lies in $U$. Their spatial derivatives are bounded there by the slice $L^2$ bound. On a compact set of centers $C$ for which the closed $\delta$-neighborhood
$K_{C,\delta}=\{y\in\R^3:\operatorname{dist}(y,C)\le\delta\}$
is compactly contained in $U$, every translated kernel test is supported in
the same compact $K_{C,\delta}$. The pairing hypothesis therefore gives the
same constants $A_{K_{C,\delta},J}$, $P_{K_{C,\delta},J}$, and
$\theta_{K_{C,\delta},J}$ for every center $x\in C$; the three test
seminorms are invariant under translation. Arzel\`a--Ascoli on compact
space-time sets and a diagonal extraction over $m$ and a compact exhaustion
therefore give locally uniform limits $g_m$ of these mollified fields. In particular, for every $x$ whose $1/m$-ball lies in $U$ and every $t\in I$, convergence of the translated-kernel pairings gives $g_m(x,t)=(u_t*\rho_{1/m})(x)$.

Extend each $g_m$ by zero to the rest of $U\times I$, and define each component of $u(x,t)$ as $\limsup_{m\to\infty}g_m(x,t)$ when this is finite, and as zero otherwise. Every $x\in U$ is in the domain of $g_m$ for all sufficiently large $m$. The local uniform limits $g_m$ are measurable, so this pointwise definition is jointly measurable. For a fixed $t$, choose a countable exhaustion $W_j\Subset V_j\Subset U$
and functions $\chi_j\in\Cc(U)$ equal to one on $V_j$. Extend
$\chi_j u_t$ by zero to $\R^3$; this extension is locally integrable on
$\R^3$ because $u_t\in L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$. Apply the Lebesgue differentiation theorem for approximate identities
whose kernels have supports of comparable inner and outer radii
componentwise to this extension and the scaled bumps with outer radius $1/m$
and inner radius $1/(2m)$. Their radius ratio is $2$, and their
integral-normalized kernels are $\rho_{1/m}$. The theorem gives a.e. spatial
convergence for each $j$ and component. For every $x\in W_j$, all
sufficiently large kernels are supported in $V_j$, where $\chi_j=1$, so
their convolutions with the extension agree with $u_t*\rho_{1/m}$. Taking
the countable union over $j$ and the components proves convergence to $u_t$
almost everywhere on $U$. The exceptional spatial null set may depend on
the fixed time $t$, as allowed by the statement.

For a compact $K\subset U$, choose a relatively compact open neighborhood $V\Subset U$ containing the $\delta$-neighborhood of $K$ for all sufficiently small $\delta$. For almost every $t$ and each shift $y$ in the kernel support, the Sobolev translation estimate on $K$ follows for smooth fields by integrating $Du_n$ along the segment from $x$ to $x-y$; approximation in $W^{1,2}(V)$ gives the same estimate for the slices. Minkowski's inequality applied to the normalized kernel, followed by integration in $t$, gives
\begin{equation*}
\norm{u_n-u_n*\rho_\delta}_{L^2(K\times J)}\le C_\rho\delta\norm{Du_n}_{L^2(\overline V\times J)}\,,
\end{equation*}
where $C_\rho$ depends only on the fixed kernel profile. The convolution and every segment used in the estimate stay inside $V$, so no boundary extension enters the estimate. The right side tends to zero uniformly in $n$. At each fixed scale the mollified subsequence converges locally uniformly, hence in $L^2(K\times J)$. The translation estimate then makes $(u_{n_k})$ Cauchy in $L^2(K\times J)$, so it converges strongly there to some $v$. A further subsequence converges in $L^2(K)$ for almost every $t$; its every-time weak limit is $u_t$, so $v(\cdot,t)=u_t$ for almost every $t$. The slice identification above and Fubini's theorem give $v=u$ almost everywhere in $K\times J$. This proves the asserted local strong convergence.

The gradient bounds make $(Du_{n_k})$ bounded in the separable Hilbert spaces $L^2(V_j\times J;\R^{3\times3})$ for the relatively compact open sets $V_j$ in a countable exhaustion of $U$ and compact time intervals $J$ in a countable exhaustion of $I$. Weak sequential compactness and a diagonal subsequence give a local weak limit $Du$. Passing to the limit in the distributional identities defining $Du_{n_k}$ is justified by strong convergence of $u_{n_k}$ and weak convergence of $Du_{n_k}$; it shows that $Du$ is the space-time weak spatial gradient of $u$. For each $j$, choose a countable family
$\mathcal D_j\subset\Cc(V_j;\R^3)$ dense in
$W^{1,2}_0(V_j;\R^3)$ with its $W^{1,2}$ norm. For each
$\eta\in\mathcal D_j$, test the distributional identities with
$\eta(x)\zeta(t)$ for every $\zeta\in\Cc(I)$; Fubini gives the
slice identity outside a null set that may depend on $\eta$. Since the
families are countable, their null sets have a countable union, so one
null-time set works for every test in every $\mathcal D_j$. At each
remaining time the slices of $u$ and $Du$ are locally square integrable.
Continuity of the distributional pairing in the $W^{1,2}$ norm therefore
extends the identity to every test in $\Cc(V_j;\R^3)$. Exhausting $U$
by the $V_j$ proves that $Du(\cdot,t)$ is the weak gradient of
$u(\cdot,t)$ for almost every $t$. Weak lower semicontinuity gives the gradient bound in (e). For each fixed $t$, weak lower semicontinuity of the slice norm and (a) give the stated slice bound. The continuity assertion in (d) was proved first for the dense smooth family and extends to every compactly supported $L^2$ test by approximation, uniformly in $t$ on compact intervals using the slice bound.
\end{proof}

The compactness conclusion is local in space-time. In particular, it gives weak convergence of every time slice, not strong convergence of slice norms; the latter is not needed for the weak-pairing and lower-semicontinuity arguments used below.

\begin{remark}[Departure from \cite{ESS2003}]
The local extraction here yields strong $L^2$ convergence in space-time and weak $L^2$ convergence of slices at every time. It does not assert the strong $C([a,b];L^2_{\mathrm{loc}})$ convergence in~\cite[(3.23), p.~225]{ESS2003}. In the blow-up argument, uses of that stronger topology must instead be handled with the all-time weak-pairing continuity and weak lower semicontinuity supplied by the lemma.
\end{remark}

A uniform local $L^{10/3}$ bound upgrades this convergence below the exponent $10/3$.

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:compactness-Lq}
In the setting of Lemma~\ref{lem:compactness}, suppose in addition that for every compact $Q\subset U\times I$ there is a finite constant $C_Q$ such that
\begin{equation*}
\sup_n\norm{u_n}_{L^{10/3}(Q)}\le C_Q\,.
\end{equation*}
Then, along the subsequence in Lemma~\ref{lem:compactness}, $u_{n_k}\to u$ strongly in $L^q_{\mathrm{loc}}(U\times I)$ for every $1\le q<10/3$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
On each compact space-time set, strong $L^2$ convergence has a further subsequence converging almost everywhere. Fatou's lemma therefore gives the same local $L^{10/3}$ bound for $u$, and hence a uniform $L^{10/3}$ bound for $u_{n_k}-u$. For $1\le q<2$, H\"older's inequality on finite-measure sets bounds the $L^q$ norm by the $L^2$ norm. For $2<q<10/3$, choose $\lambda\in(0,1)$ so that $1/q=\lambda/2+(1-\lambda)/(10/3)$. Interpolation gives
\begin{equation*}
\norm{u_{n_k}-u}_{L^q}\le
\norm{u_{n_k}-u}_{L^2}^{\lambda}
\norm{u_{n_k}-u}_{L^{10/3}}^{1-\lambda}\,,
\end{equation*}
which tends to zero. The case $q=2$ is Lemma~\ref{lem:compactness}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Stability of suitable weak solutions}\label{subsec:stability}

The stability result below is the closure step used for the blow-up limit. Its
uniform local energy hypothesis includes the essential-supremum bound in
Definition~\ref{def:sws}; that bound is a hypothesis of the theorem and does
not follow from the three strong or weak convergence statements alone.

\begin{theorem}[Stability of suitable weak solutions]\label{thm:stability}
Let $\Omega\subset\R^3$ be open, let $I\subset\R$ be an open interval, and
let $q>\nfs52$. For each $n\in\N$, suppose that
$(u_n,Du_n,p_n,0)$ is a suitable weak solution on $\Omega\times I$ in the
sense of Definition~\ref{def:sws}. Let
$u\colon\Omega\times I\to\R^3$,
$Du\colon\Omega\times I\to\R^{3\times3}$, and
$p\colon\Omega\times I\to\R$ be fields. Assume that for every open
$\Omega'$ with compact closure contained in $\Omega$ and every interval $J$
with compact closure contained in $I$, the following hold on
$U=\Omega'\times J$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item
\[
 \sup_n\esssup_{t\in J}\int_{\Omega'}\abs{u_n(x,t)}^2\,dx<\infty,
 \qquad
 \sup_n\int_J\int_{\Omega'}\abs{Du_n(x,t)}^2\,dx\,dt<\infty;
\]
